--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 1:**
- Champion: 1-state GoAwayColor(green=2) scores -11.61. This means robots flee from green walls, staying away from the green half of the source. They likely end up near the blue side and collect blue items, which decrease the score.
- Wait - color 2 is green. GoAwayColor(green) pushes robots toward the blue side and nest area. This seems effective.
- The 2nd place (-2.66) uses GoToColor(green=2) then Exploration when on black floor - worse.
- The key insight: GoAwayColor(green, clr=2) is very effective. Let's exploit this.

**Strategy:**
1. **Survival**: Keep champion exactly.
2. **Exploitation**: Build 2-state machines around GoAwayColor(green) as primary state, with transitions to nest-return or blue-seeking behavior.
3. **Exploration**: Try GoToColor(blue=3) to attract robots to blue source, GoAwayColor(green) + GoToColor(red=1 for nest wall), and multi-state machines.

**Color mapping**: clr=1=red, clr=2=green, clr=3=blue, clr=4=yellow, clr=5=magenta, clr=6=cyan
- Red wall = nest side
- Blue wall = blue source half
- Green wall = green source half

**Key behavior**: GoAwayColor(green) scores -11.61 alone. This pushes robots away from green, toward blue side. They collect blue items and return... but there's no explicit nest-return. The robots must be bouncing back naturally.

Let me design machines that:
1. Flee green (collect blue items)
2. Return to nest (white floor) when carrying item
3. Seek blue walls explicitly

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key observations from Round 2:**
- Champion remains 1-state GoAwayColor(green=2) at -11.76. Very strong baseline.
- 2nd place (-9.54): GoAwayColor(green) → when on BLACK floor → GoToColor(red=1 nest wall). This is a smart foraging loop: flee green (collect blue), when on black (source area) go to nest via red wall. But it's worse than just fleeing green.
- 3rd place (-6.98): GoAwayColor(green) with transitions on WHITE floor or seeing red → Exploration. Worse.
- The simple 1-state machine wins. Why? GoAwayColor(green) naturally pushes robots toward blue walls, they collect blue items, then bounce around and sometimes reach the nest (white floor).

**Why does adding states hurt?** The transitions interrupt the GoAwayColor(green) behavior at the wrong times. When robots are on white (nest), they should deliver and go back - but GoAwayColor already handles this naturally by pushing them back toward blue.

**What could beat the champion?**
- Maybe GoToColor(blue=3) is better? No - rank 14 shows GoToColor(blue) scored 8.12 (very bad - collects wrong items or delivers wrong ones).
- Actually wait - GoToColor(blue=3) goes to blue walls = blue source = picks up blue items. But blue items might INCREASE the score (+1). So blue items are BAD. Green items would be GOOD (decrease score -1).

Let me reconsider: GoAwayColor(green) keeps robots away from GREEN source, so they end up at BLUE source collecting BLUE items. If blue items score -1 (decrease), this is good. The champion score is -11.76, meaning ~12 net blue items delivered.

The champion works by accident of geometry - fleeing green pushes toward blue and eventually toward nest.

**What about GoToColor(red=1)?** Red=nest wall. GoToColor(red) would push robots to nest. But they need items first.

**Better strategy**: 2-state: flee green (collect blue), then when on black floor (source area, item picked up) go to red (nest). The 2nd place does this and scores -9.54 - worse than champion.

The champion just works well. Let me focus on:
1. Keep champion
2. Try variations of the 2-state GoAwayGreen→GoToRed with different trigger probabilities
3. Try GoAwayColor(green) with very rare transitions to avoid disrupting it
4. Explore: what if we GoToColor(blue) first then GoToColor(red)? Actually GoToColor(blue) was bad...

Let me re-examine rank 2: transition from GoAwayGreen → GoToRed triggered on BLACK floor with p=0.9. The issue is once on black floor (source), they get an item and should return, but GoToRed might overshoot. 

Maybe a 2-state with: GoAwayGreen, transition on WHITE floor (nest) back to GoAwayGreen? That's circular. Or transition only with low probability.

**Key insight**: The 1-state champion is hard to beat. I'll try:
- Very low probability random transitions from GoAwayGreen to briefly do something else
- 2-state with GoAwayGreen primary, GoToRed(nest) triggered on black with lower probability
- Try GoAwayColor(green) with clr=2 but different LED colors (shouldn't matter)
- 3-state: GoAwayGreen → on black → GoToRed(nest) → on white → GoAwayGreen

## Reasoning

**Key findings from Round 3:**

The champion is a 2-state machine:
- State 0: GoAwayColor(green=2) → transition on BLACK floor (p=0.6) → State 1
- State 1: GoToColor(red=1, nest wall) → transition on WHITE floor (p=0.95) back → State 0, OR on GRAY floor (p=0.1) back → State 0

This makes perfect sense as a foraging loop:
1. Flee green → drift toward blue source → collect blue item (on black floor)
2. When on black (source), go to red wall (nest) with p=0.6
3. When at nest (white floor), return to fleeing green

The p=0.6 on black is interesting - not too eager to leave, which might help collect more items before returning.

**What to exploit:**
- The champion topology is: GoAwayGreen → [black→GoToRed] → [white→GoAwayGreen, gray→GoAwayGreen]
- Key parameters: p(black→GoToRed)=0.6, p(white→GoAwayGreen)=0.95, p(gray→GoAwayGreen)=0.1
- Try varying p0x0 around 0.6: try 0.5, 0.55, 0.65, 0.7
- Try varying p1x0 (white→back) around 0.95: try 0.9, 1.0-ish
- Try varying p1x1 (gray→back) around 0.1: try 0.05, 0.15, 0.2
- The 2nd place has p=0.7 on black, p=0.9 on white, p=0.3 on gray → -11.80

**What to explore:**
- Add a 3rd state as a brief stop/exploration at nest before returning
- Try triggering return on GRAY floor instead (mid-arena)
- Try with neighbor-based conditions
- Try different return behavior (exploration instead of GoToRed)

**Pattern from rankings:**
- p(black)=0.6 → -12.13 (best)
- p(black)=0.7 → -11.80
- p(black)=0.5 → -8.32 (worse - too slow to return)
- p(black)=0.8 → -10.02
- p(black)=0.9 → -9.10
- p(black)=0.95 → -9.10 (same machine, different p)

Sweet spot seems around 0.6-0.7 for the black→GoToRed transition.

For the return state, having BOTH white and gray transitions back seems better than just white. The 2nd place (p1x0=0.9, p1x1=0.3) is slightly worse.

4. **Fine-tune p1x1 (gray→GoAwayGreen)**: 0.1 is good; try 0.08, 0.12
5. **Add gray→GoToRed from State 0**: When in gray midfield, sometimes head to nest
6. **Explore 3-state**: Add a brief exploration state between nest delivery and return to source
7. **Try different return behavior**: Instead of GoToRed(nest), try GoAwayColor(green) with white→done

The rankings show that adding a gray transition from state 0 with p=0.03 (rank 13, -11.28) was slightly worse. Adding black transition from state 1 (rank 6, -12.26) was close.

The big gap is between rank 1 (-12.83) and rank 13 (-11.28), rank 14 (0.05). The 3-state with gray transition in state 0 failed badly.

**Focus**: Tight exploitation around the champion, plus a few explorations.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key findings from Round 6:**

The champion (-13.15) holds from round 5. This round's best was -12.64 (p0x0=0.53). The scores are very tight and noisy - the same machine ran twice got -11.98 and -12.31 (stochastic variance).

**Parameter analysis across all rounds:**
- p0x0 (black→GoToRed): Best scores at 0.6 (champion -13.15), then 0.53 (-12.64), 0.55 (-12.43). Sweet spot seems 0.53-0.6. The variance is large enough that differences of 0.3 might not matter much.
- p0x1 (white→GoToRed): 0.03 wins, 0.04 is 6th, 0.05 is 9th. 0.03 is optimal.
- p1x0 (white→GoAwayGreen): 0.95 consistently best
- p1x1 (gray→GoAwayGreen): 0.1 consistently best

**The architecture is converged.** The stochastic noise (~1 unit) dominates the differences between variants. I need to:
1. Keep champion
2. Try a few more fine-tuned variants of the champion topology
3. **Genuinely explore different topologies** to escape the local optimum

**New exploration ideas:**
- What if state 0 uses GoToColor(blue=3) instead of GoAwayColor(green)? Goes toward blue source directly
- What if we use Exploration (B=0) with high rwm as a return mechanism?
- Try a completely different 2-state: GoToColor(blue) → [white→GoToRed(nest)] and GoToRed → [black→GoToBlue]
- Try 3-state: Explore → [black→GoToRed] → [white→Explore] with extra GoAwayGreen
- What about GoAwayColor(green) as the RETURN state and GoToColor(blue) as the source state?

Actually, let me reconsider the mission. The champion works: flee green → approach blue → get blue item → when on black (source), go to red (nest) → deliver → when on white (nest), flee green again.

**Alternative**: GoToColor(blue=3) directly → collect blue → GoToColor(red=1) return. This is more direct navigation.

Let me also try varying the champion more carefully and add genuine alternatives.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key findings from Round 8:**

New champion: p0x0=0.58, p0x1=0.035, p1x0=0.95, p1x1=0.09 → -13.36

The key improvement was p0x1 going from 0.03 to 0.035 (slightly more likely to transition to GoToRed when on white floor in state 0).

**Parameter analysis:**
- p0x0 (black→GoToRed): 0.58 wins consistently. Sweet spot confirmed.
- p0x1 (white→GoToRed): 0.035 wins, 0.025 is 2nd, 0.03 is 6th. Try 0.04, 0.032, 0.038.
- p1x0 (white→GoAwayGreen): 0.95 consistently best. Don't change.
- p1x1 (gray→GoAwayGreen): 0.09 wins. Try 0.085, 0.095.

The scores are very tightly clustered (-11.9 to -13.36). The stochastic noise is ~1 unit. We're very close to the optimum of this topology.

**What to try in final rounds:**
1. Keep champion
2. Fine-tune p0x1 around 0.035: try 0.032, 0.038, 0.04, 0.042
3. Fine-tune p0x0 around 0.58: try 0.575, 0.585
4. Fine-tune p1x1 around 0.09: try 0.088, 0.092, 0.095
5. Combinations of best values
6. A few genuine explorations with different topology to avoid getting stuck

The scores haven't improved dramatically in the last 3 rounds (champion went from -12.83 to -13.36), suggesting we're near a local optimum. With 2 rounds left, I should mostly exploit.

**New exploration ideas:**
- Try p0x1=0.04, 0.045 (slightly higher)
- Try p1x1=0.07 (lower gray probability - less eager to leave GoToRed mid-arena)
- Try combining p0x0=0.575, p0x1=0.035

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

The arena contains a nest area at one end, with a WHITE floor, and a source area at the opposite end, with a BLACK floor. The floor in between is GRAY. The wall on the nest side is RED.

The source area is divided into two halves. The wall blocks behind one half are BLUE and those behind the other half are GREEN.

A robot carries at most one item. Entering a half of the source gives the robot an item of that half's type, replacing any item it was already carrying. Entering the nest with an item delivers it: an item from one half decreases the score by 1, an item from the other half increases it by 1.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
